<!-- PM:END pm.objects.v2 -->

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-block-mass-lower | W | ≥ | $w$ | The block-mass parameter is fixed before taking the denominator tail. |
| q-block-mass-upper | W | ≤ | $2w$ | The upper mass bound follows from the stopping overshoot after individual summands become small. |
| q-mask-law | \pi_L(d) | = | $\frac{\phi(L/d)}L$ | The law weights deficit masks and does not itself count numerator residues. |
| q-density-exponent | H_0 | = | $.001$ | An absolute exponent fixed before the small popularity cutoff. |
| q-centre-density-ceiling | \delta_L | ≤ | $1$ | The density is the assigned totient sum divided by the host. |
| q-adjusted-centre-log-scale | \|\log k_{L_i}\| | ≤ | $B_i^*+1$ | The shell controls the absolute logarithmic spatial scale. |
| q-adjusted-centre-positive-log-scale | \log k_{L_i} | ≤ | $.1(B_i^*+1)$ | The positive logarithmic scale has the stronger cost bound. |
| q-retained-centre-density-floor | \delta_L | ≥ | $e^{-.32B_i^*}$ | The lower density bound precedes subsequent mask and numerator removals. |
| q-geometric-gap-exponent | \sigma_g | = | $.1$ | Chosen during the prime-power and geometry stage. |
| q-hard-birth-mean-floor | mean hard birth weight | ≥ | $c_{\rm hard}R(VP_M/m)$ | The lower constant is independent of the final small cutoff once that cutoff is sufficiently small. |
| q-hard-birth-mean-ceiling | mean hard birth weight | ≤ | $R(VP_M/m)$ | The upper mean is the baseline totient density of the non-tail projected denominator. |
| q-virtual-first-mass-floor | virtual first mass | ≥ | $c_{\rm hard}\phi(v)r(v)$ | The virtual product omits the specified discard tests while retaining the realized adaptive fields. |
| q-virtual-first-mass-ceiling | virtual first mass | ≤ | $\phi(v)r(v)$ | The virtual first mass is bounded above by assigned row first mass. |
| q-extracted-box-lower-capacity | D_i | ≥ | $\eta^{2+\vartheta}$ | Applies to the capacities of the captured dyadic box after the saving alternative fails. |
| q-extracted-box-upper-capacity | D_i | ≤ | $\eta^{-\vartheta}$ | Both capacities obey the upper threshold in the structured alternative. |
| q-later-omission-factor | later projection congruence averaging factor | = | $1/b_i^+$ | The factor belongs to the original location’s later projection congruences and multiplies across mapped sides. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-nonvanishing-width-reduction** [claim](#stmt-pre-large-widths) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-large-widths): Mean convergence of bounded periodic interval indicators on fixed measurable sets. -> Full measure in the nonvanishing-width case.
- **step-select-late-finite-blocks** [claim](#stmt-pre-blocks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-blocks): Stop divergent partial sums after individual terms become small. -> Disjoint late finite blocks with controlled mass.
- **step-elementary-arithmetic** [claim](#stmt-pre-elementary-prime-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-elementary-prime-bounds): Binomial prime-product bounds, partial summation, and Euler factorization. -> Prime-log, reciprocal-prime, Euler-product, and small-power divisor bounds.
- **step-prime-coordinate-concentration** [claim](#stmt-ar-pivot) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ar-pivot): Convolution estimates identify dominant atoms and absorb off-pivot mass. -> Kernel excess and common-pivot concentration bounds.
- **step-tensorize-integer-kernel** [claim](#stmt-ar-tensor) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ar-tensor): Iterated prime-coordinate inequalities followed by monotone convergence. -> The integer-family tensor estimate.
- **step-determinant-cycle-counts** [claim](#stmt-ar-cycle) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ar-cycle): Bezout solutions, complete residue enumeration, and periodic interval decomposition. -> Determinant-cycle means, periods, and progression roundoff.
- **step-host-density** [claim](#stmt-cen-density) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-density): Compare the host with its double and use failure of popularity. -> Upper bounds on assigned-row density.
- **step-centre-kernel-and-raw-rows** [claim](#stmt-cen-kernel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-kernel): Convert densities to mass powers, apply the tensor estimate, and convolve dyadic widths. -> Superlinear centre-kernel summation and the raw-row second-moment alternative.
- **step-adjusted-hosts** [claim](#stmt-cen-adjusted) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-adjusted): Minimize assignment cost, count source boxes, and purge small densities. -> Controlled adjusted scales, object counts, and retained lower densities.
- **step-exterior-and-decay** [claim](#stmt-cen-exterior) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-exterior): Compare assignment costs across bands and reserve width or height decay for summation. -> Exterior separation and summable width-height kernels.
- **step-good-mask-purge** [claim](#stmt-pre-mask-purge) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-mask-purge): Prime-coordinate moments and Markov bounds under the unrestricted deficit law. -> Negligible total and centre-relative mask failure.
- **step-rational-models-and-hard-means** [claim](#stmt-pre-hard-mean) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-hard-mean): Rational separation, tagged reconstruction, and primewise residue means. -> Model uniqueness and uniform hard first-mass bounds.
- **step-soft-test-loss** [claim](#stmt-pre-soft-loss) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pre-soft-loss): Bound expected logarithmic gcd under hard conditioning, then apply Markov and a union bound. -> An arbitrarily small relative soft-test loss.
- **step-prime-matching** [claim](#stmt-tab-prime-update) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tab-prime-update): Fractional plus-minus matching and common translation-orbit averaging. -> Cap preservation and exact first-mass preservation at a sparse update.
- **step-variance-budget** [claim](#stmt-tab-variance-budget) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tab-variance-budget): Identify update increments as orbit variances and telescope capped squared class masses. -> A finite budget for update variance and class coarsening.
- **step-grid-sieve** [claim](#stmt-ar-sieve) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ar-sieve): Minimize a Selberg square majorant and control determinant-cycle roundoff. -> Small-prime exclusion bounds for close grid pairs.
- **step-rotation-alternative** [claim](#stmt-ar-rotation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ar-rotation): Pigeonhole rational approximation, denominator-range decomposition, and sieving prime returns. -> A rational approximation or sparse integer and prime returns.
- **step-weighted-lcm** [claim](#stmt-ar-lcm) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ar-lcm): Prime stripping, common pivots, totient capacities, and dyadic moment summation. -> The weighted lcm estimate and its fixed-exponent cell form.
- **step-small-scale-extraction** [claim](#stmt-ar-extraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ar-extraction): Maximal-quality cells, coordinate entropy budgets, exceptional-pattern selection, and dyadic capacities. -> A saturated structured box with controlled capacities and fixed ratio factors.
- **step-box-comparison-sampling** [claim](#stmt-ar-box-sampling) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ar-box-sampling): Dominate vertex measures by unrestricted sampling laws and apply a second-moment inequality. -> Negligible event-mass and vertex-restriction bounds.
- **step-ultra-switch-and-fold** [claim](#stmt-dir-ultra-switch) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dir-ultra-switch): Switch divisors and primes, assemble rational approximants, and average high tag digits. -> Coupled models and identical-or-disjoint tag arrangements with folded coefficients.
- **step-bill-exact-and-ultra** [claim](#stmt-dir-exact-equalities) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dir-exact-equalities): Hilbert-space Jensen averaging, soft-test partner counts, and a unique-signature count. -> Linear exact-equality cost and the complete ultra comparison bound.
- **step-nonexact-row-kernel** [claim](#stmt-dir-row-kernel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dir-row-kernel): Average common digits, bound forbidden-colour coincidences, and sieve small mismatch primes. -> A weighted nonexact folded row kernel.
- **step-static-hole-cost** [claim](#stmt-dir-holes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dir-holes): Count heavy pivot incidences and bound hard-conditioned rational-neighbourhood probabilities. -> Arbitrarily small birth loss using squarefree low moduli.
- **step-exclude-saturated-birth-boxes** [claim](#stmt-dir-deep-box) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dir-deep-box): Separate high, shallow medium, and deep medium cases; use tag contradictions, host density, static holes, and broad-model recognition. -> No residual saturated birth configuration and a summable determinant-scale saving.
- **step-direct-birth-input** [claim](#stmt-dir-birth-comparisons) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dir-deep-box-2): Combine ultra billing, static-hole costs, and residual small-scale savings. -> The direct simultaneous-birth portion of the first-sharing input.
- **step-sparse-estimates-and-trains** [claim](#stmt-sp-sparse-estimates) / [proof](#sec:sparse): Bare determinant counts, switching, dense-bin neutralization, off-gap prime windows, and deterministic quantile marks. -> Sparse-alignment bounds and deterministic trains covering accepted coarsening segments.
- **step-target-grid-and-mask-counts** [claim](#stmt-part-target-grid) / [proof](#proof-stmt-part-target-grid): Use processed upper fields, reverse orbit averaging, low periods, and primewise mask sums. -> Target-grid proximity bounds and conditional mask-currency estimates.
- **step-history-tests-and-hulls** [claim](#stmt-part-history-test-cost) / [proof](#proof-stmt-part-history-test-cost): Separate threatening and nonthreatening histories, retain assignments when necessary, and protect historical connecting chains. -> Small history-test cost and inherited hulls contained in old danger unions.
- **step-construct-padded-partitions** [claim](#stmt-tab-partition-input) / [proof](#pm-h-66): Sample periodic microinterval boundaries outside fixed inherited hulls, then apply padding and directional gap tests. -> Core-equivariant cells preserving classes and old-child cell separation with small expected loss.
- **step-classify-first-joins** [claim](#stmt-join-classification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-join-classification): Compare current and preceding adjacency thresholds and use band separation and dense-height starts. -> Distant or mixed first-join alternatives.
- **step-backward-prefix-and-endpoint-counts** [claim](#stmt-join-backward-decomposition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-join-backward-decomposition): Expand actual adaptive prefixes backwards, continue only ordinary product terms, and stop covariance terms separately. -> Continuing and stopped joint-loss branches with controlled endpoint periods.
- **step-later-omissions-and-capacity** [claim](#stmt-join-capacity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-join-capacity): Average original-grid projection congruences before summing link-matrix column capacities. -> Image-pair charges without extra origin-mask or epoch multiplicity.
- **step-complete-remaining-first-joins** [claim](#stmt-join-remaining-first-joins) / [proof](#pm-h-73): Restore first-mass main terms and sum distant, width-separated, and oriented mixed roundoff. -> The remaining broad and narrow first-sharing comparison bounds.
- **step-close-chronological-inputs** [claim](#stmt-tab-first-join-input) / [proof](#proof-stmt-join-capacity-2): Finite chronological induction using past histories for partitions and existing partitions for sparse updates. -> All comparison and partition inputs for the conditional reduction.
- **step-final-broad-energy** [claim](#stmt-tab-energy-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tab-energy-bound): Orbit expansion, tight signed cancellation, capacity bounds, and convolution across widths. -> A uniform final broad-energy bound.
- **step-virtual-and-intermediate-fields** [claim](#stmt-tab-virtual-marginals) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tab-virtual-marginals): Use realized adaptive multipliers with scalar extensions, reverse orbit averages, and a finite discard telescoping identity. -> Dominating virtual products, exact discard currency, subset means, periods, and intermediate upper fields.
- **step-relative-equidistribution** [claim](#stmt-tab-equidistribution) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tab-equidistribution): Truncate to small-prime fields, count complete periods, and compare full and truncated means relatively. -> Uniform relative equidistribution on fixed intervals.
- **step-transfer-and-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-tab-conditional-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tab-conditional-reduction): Approximate a fixed avoided set by a finite interval union and compare virtual first mass, discard loss, and retained second moment. -> No positive-measure tail-avoided set and the main weak approximation theorem.
- **step-hausdorff-mass-transference** [claim](#stmt-cor-hausdorff) / [proof](#proof-stmt-join-capacity-3): Apply the main theorem to gauge-transformed radii, restrict to a locally exhaustive enumeration, and transfer Lebesgue fullness to Hausdorff fullness. -> The Hausdorff-measure divergence corollary.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-prime-cell-cap**: The prescribed prime matching update preserves the raw cell-load cap for each after-prime mask.
- **invariant-prime-label-marginals**: Averaging the simultaneous prime translations preserves each transported label’s weighted mass and hence the cell’s first mass.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-independent-success-colours**: Replace simultaneous translations by independently sampled successes for different plus entries. -> This changes joint outcomes and does not represent the prescribed orbit, even when individual success probabilities agree.
- **anti-mask-law-squared-for-clipping**: Apply the broad product of mask coefficients to the clipping estimate. -> The resulting count uses the wrong first-mass currency for a fixed remaining mask.
- **anti-finite-orbit-certifies-main-theorem**: Treat successful finite prime-update checks as certification of the almost-everywhere approximation theorem. -> The finite matching calculation does not establish the comparison estimates, chronological partition construction, or passage through arbitrarily late blocks.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The prime matching rule is a finite construction: product coupling specifies a matching matrix, and the common prime translations form a finite orbit. The executable scope is this local operation from the mass-and-cap preservation lemma; it does not certify the infinite approximation theorem.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON with prime p, positive exponent nu, rational cap, plus entries carrying weight and residue, and minus weights.
# PRECONDITIONS: Each raw weight lies in [0,1]; both incoming list loads are at most cap; residues lie in range(p).
# OUTPUTS: Exact rational plus and minus raw arrival weights for every common translation t in range(p).
# TIME: O(p*(len(plus)*len(minus)+len(plus)+len(minus))) rational operations, excluding validation.
# SPACE: O(p*(len(plus)+len(minus))) rational values.
# ARITHMETIC: Fractions provide exact rational arithmetic; primality is checked exactly.
# FAILURE: Invalid input raises an exception and exits unsuccessfully.
import json
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
from sympy import isprime

def main():
    data = json.load(sys.stdin)
    p = data['p']
    nu = data['nu']
    if type(p) is not int or not isprime(p) or type(nu) is not int or nu < 1:
        raise ValueError('invalid prime power')
    cap = Fraction(data['cap'])
    if cap < 0:
        raise ValueError('negative cap')
    plus = []
    for entry in data['plus']:
        weight = Fraction(entry['weight'])
        residue = entry['residue']
        if not 0 <= weight <= 1 or type(residue) is not int or not 0 <= residue < p:
            raise ValueError('invalid plus entry')
        plus.append((weight, residue))
    minus = [Fraction(value) for value in data['minus']]
    if any(not 0 <= weight <= 1 for weight in minus):
        raise ValueError('invalid minus entry')
    if sum((w for w, _ in plus), Fraction(0)) > cap or sum(minus, Fraction(0)) > cap:
        raise ValueError('incoming cap exceeded')
    R = Fraction(p - 1, p)
    scale = R if nu >= 2 else Fraction(1)
    scaled = [weight * scale for weight, _ in plus]
    P = sum(scaled, Fraction(0))
    M = sum(minus, Fraction(0))
    matched = min(P, M)
    links = [[matched * a * b / (P * M) if P and M else Fraction(0) for b in minus] for a in scaled]
    outcomes = []
    for t in range(p):
        out_plus = []
        out_minus = [Fraction(0) for _ in minus]
        for i, (_, residue) in enumerate(plus):
            success = (residue + t) % p == 0
            out_plus.append(scaled[i] if success else Fraction(0))
            if not success:
                for j in range(len(minus)):
                    out_minus[j] += links[i][j]
        for j, weight in enumerate(minus):
            used = sum((links[i][j] for i in range(len(plus))), Fraction(0))
            out_minus[j] += (weight - used) * R
        outcomes.append({'plus': [str(x) for x in out_plus], 'minus': [str(x) for x in out_minus]})
    json.dump({'outcomes': outcomes}, sys.stdout, sort_keys=True)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
```

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |

| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The full-measure conclusion does not provide a terminating procedure that decides approximation for a supplied real point or tolerance sequence. |

| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Recognition of rational models uses prescribed phase and width thresholds; the paper gives no computational representation or comparison procedure for arbitrary real phases. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The finite prime matching rule constructs a fractional coupling between weighted plus and minus lists. Given exact endpoint weights and success indicators, its retention rule preserves the cell cap; first-mass preservation additionally requires averaging over the prescribed translation orbits. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Accounting classes give a concrete finite representation of historical equivalence: surviving descendants retain their equivalence even after an intermediate connecting label disappears. New partitions must contain these inherited classes. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The tensor kernel is an arithmetic inequality proved by prime-coordinate estimates and convergence. It supplies no hardware mapping, bounded precision specification, or accelerator resource contract. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Tagged numerator restrictions use congruences to control arithmetic overlap. Rational-key uniqueness and forbidden residues do not establish secrecy, unpredictability, or cryptographic hardness. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The entropy budget constrains prime-exponent distributions of weighted arithmetic edges. It does not construct a learned predictor, specify training data, or prove a learning guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Centre assignment minimizes a particular arithmetic cost over popular centres in a finite block. The paper supplies no reduction from an operational optimisation problem or complexity guarantee for finding these centres. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Chronological partitioning relies on a prescribed global order, inherited geometric history, and current-core equivariance. No protocol for asynchronous execution, communication failures, or replica agreement is constructed. |
| other (Exact finite probability models on integer divisors) | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The deficit law constructs an explicit finite probability distribution on divisor masks. Its prime coordinates factor independently, giving a concrete arithmetic model whose weights describe denominator deficits. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-fixed-shift-and-finite-tolerances** (hypothesis): The main theorem fixes a real shift and requires a finite-valued nonnegative tolerance function. It does not assert simultaneous validity outside a common exceptional set for all shifts and tolerance functions. Blocked use: ['Allow the shift to vary with the denominator.', 'Claim a universal exceptional null set independent of the prescribed data.', 'Apply the theorem to infinite tolerance values.']
- **limit-required-totient-divergence** (hypothesis): The sufficient divergence criterion uses totient weights. Unweighted divergence alone does not justify the conclusion for arbitrary nonmonotone tolerances. Blocked use: ['Replace the totient-weighted divergence hypothesis by unweighted divergence on an arbitrary sparse support.']
- **limit-unrestricted-numerators** (formal_scope): The approximation conclusion permits unrestricted numerators. It does not establish the stronger coprime-numerator assertion. Blocked use: ['Impose ordinary numerator coprimality while retaining the claimed arbitrary-fixed-shift conclusion.']
- **limit-no-converse-or-frequency** (formal_scope): The main result supplies a sufficient divergence statement, without a convergence converse or quantitative counting asymptotic. Blocked use: ['Infer null measure from convergence of the totient-weighted series.', 'Derive a finite waiting-time bound or an asymptotic number of successful denominators.']
- **limit-hausdorff-gauge-regularity** (hypothesis): The Hausdorff consequence requires the stated continuity, nondecreasing behaviour, decay at the origin, and monotonicity of the gauge-to-radius ratio, together with its own divergence condition. Blocked use: ['Transfer Lebesgue fullness to an arbitrary gauge without checking the gauge hypotheses.', 'Use the original weighted divergence condition in place of the gauge-weighted condition.']
- **limit-small-width-block-reduction** (hypothesis): The late fixed-mass block construction is used after separating the nonvanishing-width case. Its stopping argument relies on divergence and eventual smallness of individual mass terms. Blocked use: ['Assert the same arbitrarily late block selection for a convergent series.', 'Treat block existence as an explicit uniform bound on where a block begins.']
- **limit-centre-density-assignment-scope** (hypothesis): The centre-density estimate requires common-width rows dividing the proposed centre and the original-host comparison for every nonraw assigned row. The retained lower density bounds concern original assignments before subsequent removals. Blocked use: ['Apply the density estimate to arbitrary denominator families.', 'Assume the retained density floor still holds after arbitrary mask or numerator deletions.']
- **limit-parameter-order-and-mask-purge** (hypothesis): Negligible good-mask deletion depends on the prescribed order of fixed parameter choices and the final small-cutoff regime. It is not an unconditional loss bound for arbitrary thresholds. Blocked use: ['Choose goodness thresholds independently of the hierarchy.', 'Interpret the asymptotic purge estimate as an explicit numerical loss certificate for a supplied block.']
- **limit-mask-law-not-numerator-law** (formal_scope): The deficit law weights row denominators. Numerator-residue frequencies require a separate complete-period or translation-orbit argument, and the fixed shift is never randomized. Blocked use: ['Use a mask coefficient as an automatic probability of a numerator exclusion.', 'Infer randomness of the shift from auxiliary mask sampling.']
- **limit-rational-recognition-and-soft-conditioning** (hypothesis): Rational recognition is restricted to the specified type, tier, width, and height conditions. The soft-test loss is relative to normalized hard birth weights. Blocked use: ['Apply model uniqueness to arbitrary rational reconstructions outside the recognition thresholds.', 'Transfer the soft-loss probability to an unrelated numerator distribution.']
- **limit-prime-update-orbit-averaging** (formal_scope): Prime matching preserves weighted mass after averaging the prescribed simultaneous translations. The result does not require independent success colours and does not assert pointwise mass preservation at every orbit position. Blocked use: ['Replace the common translation orbit by independent successes.', 'Claim that each realized individual label keeps its pre-update mass.']
- **limit-pair-currencies** (formal_scope): Broad comparisons use both mask-law coefficients even for identical masks. Clipping instead uses a single coefficient at a common width and remaining mask. Blocked use: ['Square the remaining-mask coefficient in a clipping bound.', 'Replace the product-law broad coefficient by a single coefficient merely because the masks agree.']
- **limit-chronology-and-inherited-history** (hypothesis): Operational partitions must preserve inherited equivalences without splitting and use previously generated history. Removing a connecting label does not erase its historical equivalence or stored connecting segments. Blocked use: ['Rebuild accounting classes solely from currently surviving adjacency.', 'Use a future partition to justify an earlier sparse-step estimate.', 'Resample boundaries after changing the preliminary hulls.']
- **limit-cap-dependent-energy** (hypothesis): The final broad-energy bound assumes the comparison and partition inputs. Its constant may depend on the fixed cap and hierarchy, even though the comparison constants used to choose the cap are independent of it. Blocked use: ['Treat the broad-energy proposition as unconditional before establishing its inputs.', 'Advertise the final energy bound as uniform over arbitrarily varying caps.']
- **limit-virtual-products-and-fixed-intervals** (formal_scope): Virtual domination is asserted after static anchor averaging. Equidistribution concerns fixed nondegenerate spatial intervals in the denominator-tail limit. Blocked use: ['Demand domination separately for every anchor choice.', 'Use fixed-interval equidistribution as a uniform estimate for shrinking or adaptively chosen intervals.']
- **limit-lcm-inflation-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The weighted lcm estimate requires totient-bounded vertex capacities, the stated edge majorant, an lcm cutoff, and the surrounding moment assumptions on multiplicative inflation. Blocked use: ['Apply the lcm bound to arbitrary weighted graphs.', 'Allow uncontrolled multiplicative inflation while keeping the same uniform constant.']
- **limit-extraction-is-an-alternative** (formal_scope): Small-scale extraction provides either a saving or a structured subfamily under the normalized graph hypotheses. It does not guarantee a structured box for every input graph. Blocked use: ['Require extraction to return a saturated box when the saving alternative holds.', 'Apply the capacity conclusions outside the sufficiently small determinant-scale regime.']
- **limit-auxiliary-sampling-independence** (hypothesis): The saturated-box sampling statement bounds event mass under unrestricted comparison laws. Varying an integer factor while fixing an auxiliary phase requires separately justified independence or a uniform conditional sampling law. Blocked use: ['Assume the extracted surviving edge distribution has independent coordinates.', 'Freeze an auxiliary phase that depends on the factor being switched.']
- **limit-static-holes-and-danger-train-supply** (hypothesis): The additional rational holes are specified from aggregate good-row lists before adaptive tables. Stored danger history includes the full prescribed supply, including grids unused by actual arrivals. Blocked use: ['Define the static hole rule retrospectively from a selected extracted box.', 'Reduce stored history to trains supporting currently surviving edges.']
- **limit-capacity-charge-order** (hypothesis): First-join capacity bounds apply after averaging the original locations’ later projection congruences. Link capacities preserve the relevant mask data and charge image pairs at their first-sharing epoch. Blocked use: ['Insert an additional sum over origin masks after using matching column capacities.', 'Charge the same image pair repeatedly at later comparison epochs.', 'Replace the clipping coefficient by its square during transport.']
- **limit-finite-check-versus-infinite-theorem** (nonconstructive): An exact finite matching check concerns a local construction. The full-measure theorem additionally uses tail limits, measurable-set approximation, comparison estimates, and chronological partition construction. Blocked use: ['Treat a finite prime-update certificate as a certificate of the full approximation theorem.', 'Infer individual real-point membership in the infinite limsup from finitely many test cases.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


</td>
</tr></tbody>
<tbody><tr>
<td>

&emsp;[The Weak Inhomogeneous Duffin–Schaeffer Conjecture

We prove the weak inhomogeneous Duffin–Schaeffer conjecture. For every fixed <i>γ</i> ∈ ℝ and finite-valued $`\psi:\mathbb N\to[0,\infty)`$, divergence of $`\sum_{q\ge1}\phi(q)\psi(q)/q`$ implies $`\|qx-\gamma\|\lt \psi(q)`$ for infinitely many <i>q</i>, for almost every <i>x</i>. Here <i>ϕ</i> is Euler's totient and $`\|\cdot\|`$ is distance to the nearest integer. Numerators need not be reduced, and the shift satisfies no Diophantine restriction.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For a real number $t$, write $\|t\|$ for its distance to the nearest integer, and let $\phi$ denote Euler’s totient function. We study approximation with a fixed additive shift and an arbitrary sequence of tolerances. The exceptional null set in the result below is allowed to depend on both of these data.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Weak inhomogeneous Duffin–Schaeffer). *Let $\gamma\in\mathbb R$ be fixed, and let $\psi:\mathbb N\to[0,\infty)$ be finite-valued. If <a id="eq:main-divergence"></a>$$\label{eq:main-divergence}
  \sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\frac{\phi(q)}q\,\psi(q)=\infty,$$ then, for Lebesgue-almost every $x\in\mathbb R$, the inequality $$\|qx-\gamma\|<\psi(q)$$ holds for infinitely many positive integers $q$.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves the weak inhomogeneous Duffin–Schaeffer conjecture positively, including every prescribed irrational shift. The integer nearest to $qx-\gamma$ is unrestricted: no coprimality condition is imposed on the numerator. The conclusion is a divergence statement, not a convergence converse or a quantitative asymptotic.

The mass transference principle of Beresnevich and Velani gives the following Hausdorff-measure consequence.

<a id="stmt-cor-hausdorff"></a>
<div id="cor:hausdorff" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Hausdorff-measure divergence). *Fix $\gamma\in\mathbb R$ and a finite-valued $\psi:\mathbb N\to[0,\infty)$. Let $f:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)$ be continuous and nondecreasing, with $f(r)\to0$ as $r\downarrow0$, and suppose that $f(r)/r$ is monotone. Set $f(0)=0$ and $$W_\gamma(\psi)=\{x\in\mathbb R:
    \|qx-\gamma\|<\psi(q)\text{ for infinitely many }q\}.$$ If $$\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\phi(q)f\!\left(\frac{\psi(q)}q\right)=\infty,$$ then, for every bounded interval $I$, $$\mathcal H^f(I\cap W_\gamma(\psi))=\mathcal H^f(I),$$ where $\mathcal H^f$ denotes Hausdorff $f$-measure.*

</div>

The corollary is a sufficient divergence criterion for each fixed shift, with unrestricted numerators. It requires no monotonicity of $\psi$ and asserts neither a convergence converse nor a coprime-numerator version. Its proof is given in Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">11</a>.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Background and prior work

The starting point is the monotone theory of metric approximation. Khintchine’s theorem (Khintchine 1924) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Khintchine1924](#ref-Khintchine1924)], in its usual modern form, gives a full-measure conclusion for $\|qx\|<\psi(q)$ when $\psi$ is non-increasing and $\sum_q\psi(q)$ diverges. Szüsz extended the monotone theory to each fixed inhomogeneous shift (Szüsz 1958) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Szusz1958](#ref-Szusz1958)]; see also the precise modern formulation in (Yu 2021) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Yu2021](#ref-Yu2021)]. Removing monotonicity changes the problem because many denominators can describe strongly overlapping approximation intervals. Duffin and Schaeffer (Duffin and Schaeffer 1941) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[DS1941](#ref-DS1941)] exhibited this obstruction in the homogeneous setting and proposed using reduced fractions with the weighted sum $\sum_q\phi(q)\psi(q)/q$. Koukoulopoulos and Maynard (Koukoulopoulos and Maynard 2020) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KM2020](#ref-KM2020)] proved their conjecture. The weight $\phi(q)/q$ records the proportion of reduced residue classes; it also gives a meaningful sufficient divergence criterion when all numerators are allowed.

In the inhomogeneous problem, even unrestricted numerators do not make unweighted divergence sufficient. Ramírez (Ramírez 2017, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Ramirez2017](#ref-Ramirez2017)] constructed nonmonotone counterexamples for every prescribed countable collection of shifts. His constructions have finite totient-weighted sum, as noted in that paper, and thus leave the weighted question open. Chow, Hauke, Pollington and Ramírez (Chow et al. 2025, Theorem 1.5) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[CHPR2025](#ref-CHPR2025)] show that, for suitable shifts including all rational shifts, even an approximation function that is non-increasing on its support can have divergent unweighted sum and a null approximation limsup. These results explain why a proof must control arithmetic overlap on sparse supports, not merely sum the lengths of the intervals.

The fixed-shift weighted question appears explicitly in Yu (Yu 2021, Question 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Yu2021](#ref-Yu2021)] and under the name *weak inhomogeneous Duffin–Schaeffer conjecture* in Chow and Technau (Chow and Technau 2024, Conjecture 1.22) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[ChowTechnau2024](#ref-ChowTechnau2024)]. It is also Conjecture 2 of Beresnevich, Hauke and Velani (Beresnevich, Hauke, and Velani 2024) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BHV2024](#ref-BHV2024)]. The word “weak” concerns the conclusion: the integer $a$ in $|qx-a-\gamma|<\psi(q)$ is arbitrary. The stronger formulation requires $(a,q)=1$; this distinction is already explicit in Ramírez (Ramírez 2017) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Ramirez2017](#ref-Ramirez2017)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> addresses the weak formulation for every prescribed real shift.

The coprime-numerator assertion is false in general: Hauke-Treuer, Maynard and Pollington (Hauke-Treuer, Maynard, and Pollington 2026, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[HMP2026](#ref-HMP2026)] construct counterexamples for every nonzero rational shift and certain Liouville shifts. Independent work of He and Liao (He and Liao 2026, Theorems 1–2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[HL2026](#ref-HL2026)] gives counterexamples for every nonzero rational shift and for a residual set of real shifts.

Several earlier advances treat important parts of the nonmonotone problem. Yu’s Fourier approach (Yu 2019) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Yu2019](#ref-Yu2019)] gives inhomogeneous results under additional divergence assumptions, and his Erdős–Vaaler type theorem (Yu 2021, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Yu2021](#ref-Yu2021)] treats $\psi(q)=O(1/(q(\log\log q)^2))$, with divergent unweighted sum, for a class of irrational shifts containing all non-Liouville irrationals. Chow and Technau (Chow and Technau 2024, Theorem 1.23) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[ChowTechnau2024](#ref-ChowTechnau2024)] prove the weak conjecture for certain structured nonmonotone functions arising in multiplicative approximation on fibres. Those theorems place restrictions on the shift, the approximation function, or both; they do not supply the arbitrary-function, arbitrary-fixed-shift assertion used here.

Beresnevich, Hauke and Velani (Beresnevich, Hauke, and Velani 2024, Theorem 14) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BHV2024](#ref-BHV2024)] establish the weak conjecture for every rational shift. More precisely, for $\gamma=A/B$ in lowest terms with $B>0$, their solutions can be required to satisfy $(A+aB,q)=1$. This is a restriction adapted to the shift and is different from ordinary numerator coprimality $(a,q)=1$. Their work also gives irrational-shift results under additional block or approximation hypotheses, and constructs classes of suitable shifts. The remaining task addressed by Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is to retain a completely arbitrary fixed irrational shift together with arbitrarily sparse, nonmonotone approximation functions.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Ancestry of the proof methods

The arithmetic part adapts the common-pivot approach to prime valuations developed in the homogeneous Duffin–Schaeffer work of Koukoulopoulos and Maynard (Koukoulopoulos and Maynard 2020) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[KM2020](#ref-KM2020)], simplified by Green and Walker (Green and Walker 2021, Proposition 1.2 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[GW2021](#ref-GW2021)], and used in the weighted argument of Hauke-Treuer, Vazquez and Walker (Hauke-Treuer, Vazquez, and Walker 2026, Proposition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[HVW2026](#ref-HVW2026)]. In that approach, failure of an arithmetic estimate concentrates the exponents of two denominator families around one common integer. The cited common-pivot propositions concern hypothetical minimal counterexamples in their respective arguments. We prove our weighted lcm estimate locally, with its own totient capacities and multiplicative inflation hypotheses, and then prove the small-scale extraction needed for inhomogeneous overlaps. The one-coordinate concentration argument is credited again at its point of use in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>.

Two further precedents help explain the organization. Chow and Technau’s shift-reduced numerator restrictions and localized full-measure criteria (Chow and Technau 2024, Definition 1.20 and Section 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[ChowTechnau2024](#ref-ChowTechnau2024)] illustrate how numerator conditions can be adapted to inhomogeneous arithmetic. Our conditions use rational models of the projected phases and are specified independently below. Beresnevich, Hauke and Velani obtain full measure by combining uniform distribution of selected points $a/q$ with divergence or block-mass conditions and overlap bounds (Beresnevich, Hauke, and Velani 2024, Theorems 11–12) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[BHV2024](#ref-BHV2024)]. Here the transfer is proved directly for weighted finite arrays, using a virtual comparison sum and a fixed finite-union approximation to an avoided set. These are methodological comparisons; all the construction and transfer estimates used in this paper are proved here.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy and reusable estimates

Put $r(q)=\psi(q)/q$. A row is the periodic array of points $(z+\gamma)/q$, $z\in\mathbb Z$, with spatial half-width $r(q)$. The proof works on arbitrarily late finite collections of rows whose totient-weighted mass lies in a fixed small interval. On each collection we construct a nonnegative weighted sum of interval indicators supported inside the original approximation intervals. The construction changes weights through prescribed prime-step updates and additional deletions. For comparison, we omit only these additional deletions, keeping the same realized prime-step update factors. This comparison sum is equidistributed on fixed spatial intervals; the expected discarded mass is small, and the retained sum has a uniform second-moment bound. These bounds contradict the existence of a positive-measure set avoiding all sufficiently late rows: approximate that set by one fixed finite union of intervals, then use the second moment to control the approximation error. Proposition <a href="#tab:conditional-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:conditional-reduction">33</a> gives this transfer after the construction is defined.

There are two different obstructions to the second-moment bound. First, nearness of two shifted points constrains an integer combination of their numerators to an interval too short for direct averaging. The inverse estimate of Proposition <a href="#ar:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ar:extraction">37</a> extracts denominators concentrated near a common integer in prime-exponent coordinates. Varying permitted factors then either bounds the overlap weight by a rotation estimate or forces precise rational approximations to the grid offsets. Section <a href="#sec:direct" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:direct">7</a> treats both alternatives. Exact modeled coincidences have total weight bounded linearly by the block’s totient-weighted mass, using explicit restrictions on numerator gcds.

Second, large common primes can create correlated alignments repeatedly as different collections of rows begin to interact. Each row is assigned an integer multiple of its denominator, called its centre. We expose the prime factors of centres in an increasing sequence of scales. Before all factors have been exposed, the row is represented by a finer projected grid containing its eventual true points; its labels retain the original row and the unexposed divisor data. At each prime step, short spatial cells group projected points of the same width. Some entries are retained only when their numerator is divisible by that prime. We pair portions of their weights with portions of other entries in the cell; when a divisibility test succeeds, the linked portion of the other weight is suppressed. The precise rule in Section <a href="#sec:tables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tables">5</a> preserves the first-moment normalization while cancelling the most tightly correlated transfers.

The remaining correlations are paid by a squared-mass accounting argument. Labels acquire equivalence classes through actual close alignments, and later partitions preserve those classes even when intermediate labels disappear. Deterministic interval grids record the old connecting segments, allowing new short-cell partitions to respect the history. At the first interaction of two previously separate collections, a backward comparison transfers covariance terms to linked points that passed their divisibility tests. The total incoming matching weight is bounded by the weight available at each such point, as proved in Lemma <a href="#join:capacity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="join:capacity">61</a>. The averages use common residue translations; they do not assume independent test outcomes or randomize the shift.

The reusable estimates are stated separately: small-scale extraction with auxiliary weights in Proposition <a href="#ar:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ar:extraction">37</a> and Corollary <a href="#ar:box-sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ar:box-sampling">38</a>, the linear bound for exact rational-model coincidences in Lemma <a href="#dir:exact-equalities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:exact-equalities">42</a>, and the variance budget for history-preserving projected arrays in Lemma <a href="#tab:variance-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:variance-budget">28</a>.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Organization

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> gives the finite-block reduction and the elementary tensor and residue estimates used to select centres. Sections <a href="#sec:centres" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:centres">3</a> and <a href="#sec:prescriptions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prescriptions">4</a> assign centres and prescribe the initial weights. Section <a href="#sec:tables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tables">5</a> gives the projected-array construction and reduces the theorem to three precisely stated comparison and partition assertions. Section <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">6</a> develops the sieve, rotation, and small-scale concentration estimates for close pairs. Section <a href="#sec:direct" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:direct">7</a> applies them to the comparisons for adjacent simultaneous insertions. Section <a href="#sec:sparse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sparse">8</a> controls alignments during a prime step, and Section <a href="#sec:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:partitions">9</a> constructs the required partitions. Section <a href="#sec:joins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:joins">10</a> proves the remaining first-interaction estimates and completes the finite induction. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">11</a> proves the Hausdorff-measure corollary.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Finite blocks and elementary conventions

We first reduce Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> to uniformly controlled tests on finite collections of rows. Throughout the proof the shift $\gamma$ is fixed. We work on $\mathbb T=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$ with normalized Lebesgue measure, using periodic lifts to $\mathbb R$ when counting pairs. In a lifted pair count the first point is counted per unit length and the second ranges over all its lifts. After the small-width reduction below, the widths are sufficiently small that the circle and lifted near-pair counts agree.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Reduction to small fixed-mass blocks

For $v\ge1$ set $r(v)=\psi(v)/v$, and let $$E_v=\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb Z}
 \bigl((z+\gamma)/v-r(v),(z+\gamma)/v+r(v)\bigr)
 \pmod1.$$ Thus $x\in E_v$ is exactly the approximation inequality in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. We discard rows of width zero.

<a id="stmt-pre-large-widths"></a>
<div id="pre:large-widths" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *If $v r(v)$ does not tend to zero, then $\limsup_v E_v$ has full measure.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-large-widths"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-large-widths</summary>

*Proof.* There are a constant $0<c<1/2$ and an unbounded sequence of $v$ for which $r(v)\ge c/v$. Let $F_v$ be the union of the row intervals of half-width $c/v$. Its indicator is bounded by one, has mean $2c$, and for every fixed interval $J\subset\mathbb T$ satisfies $$\int_J\mathbf1_{F_v}=2c|J|+O(1/v).$$ The error comes only from the two incomplete periods of length $1/v$. The same convergence holds for a fixed finite union of intervals. For a measurable set $A$, approximate $A$ in measure by such a union; the bound $0\le\mathbf1_{F_v}\le1$ gives $\int_A\mathbf1_{F_v}\to2c|A|$ along this sequence. Consequently no positive-measure set avoids a whole tail of the $F_v$, and therefore none avoids a whole tail of the $E_v$. Since the complement of a limsup is the countable union of these tail-avoiding sets, the conclusion follows. ◻

</details>

Henceforth suppose $v r(v)\to0$. Replace every positive width by a dyadic number between half its value and its value, retaining the notation $r(v)$. This preserves divergence of $\sum_v\phi(v)r(v)$ and can only shrink the approximation sets. If Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> fails, there is a measurable set $A\subset\mathbb T$ of measure $\mu>0$ that avoids every $E_v$ in some tail. Indeed the complement of the limsup is the union over $n$ of $\bigcap_{v\ge n}E_v^c$.

<a id="stmt-pre-blocks"></a>
<div id="pre:blocks" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *For every fixed $0<w\le1/2$ there are arbitrarily late, pairwise disjoint finite sets $\mathcal B$ of positive-width rows such that <a id="eq:block-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:block-mass}
 w\le W:=\sum_{v\in\mathcal B}\phi(v)r(v)\le2w.$$ Their least denominator tends to infinity, and all their spatial widths tend uniformly to zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-blocks</summary>

*Proof.* The individual terms satisfy $\phi(v)r(v)\le v r(v)\to0$. In a sufficiently late tail each is at most $w$. Starting after the previous block, stop at the first partial sum reaching $w$; divergence ensures that this occurs, and the last summand bounds the overshoot by $w$. The final assertion follows from $r(v)\to0$. ◻

</details>

All subsequent constructions are on one such finite block. Constants may depend on $\mu$ and on the fixed hierarchy parameters, but not on the block, its denominators, or its widths. We choose the hierarchy first and then take $w$ sufficiently small. The last approximation of $A$ by a union of intervals will occur only after those constants and the fixed bound on cell loads introduced in Section <a href="#sec:tables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tables">5</a> have been chosen.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Arithmetic notation and divisor laws

For positive integers $d,e$, write $(d,e)$ and $[d,e]$ for their gcd and lcm. Let $\nu_p(n)$ be the exponent of a prime $p$ in $n$, let $\tau(n)$ be the number of positive divisors, and let $\omega(n)$ be the number of distinct prime divisors. Put $$R(n)=\frac{\phi(n)}n,\qquad R_p=1-\frac1p.$$ All logarithms are natural unless a dyadic index is explicitly specified. The symbols $O$, $\ll$, and $\asymp$ have constants uniform in the varying block data; dependence on fixed parameters will be indicated when needed.

<a id="stmt-pre-deficit-law"></a>
<div id="pre:deficit-law" class="definition">

**Definition 5**. A mask on an integer $L\ge1$ is a divisor $d\mid L$. Its deficit law is <a id="eq:deficit-law"></a>$$\label{eq:deficit-law}
 \pi_L(d)=\frac{\phi(L/d)}L.$$ An exponent in $d$ records the corresponding deficit from $L$ in $L/d$.

</div>

The identity $\sum_{d\mid L}\phi(d)=L$ makes this a probability law. Euler factorization makes its prime coordinates independent. More explicitly, if $p^\nu\parallel L$, then $$\Pr(\nu_p(d)=j)=
 \begin{cases}p^{-j}R_p,&0\le j<\nu,\\p^{-\nu},&j=\nu,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
 \Pr(\nu_p(d)\ge j)=p^{-j}\quad(0\le j\le\nu).$$ These probabilities weight row denominators. They are not probabilities that a numerator lies in a residue class. Numerator counts at a fixed mask will always be justified separately by a complete period or a specified translation orbit. Other auxiliary probability laws used later are also explicit finite weights; the shift itself is never randomized.

<a id="stmt-pre-elementary-prime-bounds"></a>
<div id="pre:elementary-prime-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prime-log-sums"></a>
<a id="eq:prime-reciprocals"></a>
<a id="eq:divisor-small-power"></a>
**Lemma 6**. *Uniformly for $z\ge2$, $b\ge a\ge2$, and integers $n\ge1$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{p\le z}\log p&\ll z,
 &\sum_{p\le z}\frac{\log p}{p}&\ll\log(2z),\label{eq:prime-log-sums}\\
 \sum_{a\le p\le b}\frac1p
 &\ll \frac1{\log a}+\log\frac{\log b}{\log a},
 &\prod_{p\le z}R_p^{-1}&\ge\sum_{1\le m\le z}\frac1m.
 \label{eq:prime-reciprocals}
\end{aligned}$$ For every fixed $C,\epsilon>0$, $$\label{eq:divisor-small-power}
 \tau(n)^C+R(n)^{-C}\ll_{C,\epsilon}n^\epsilon.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pre-elementary-prime-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-pre-elementary-prime-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* The primes in $(N,2N]$ divide $\binom{2N}{N}\le4^N$. Summing over dyadic intervals gives the first bound in <a href="#eq:prime-log-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-log-sums">[eq:prime-log-sums]</a>; partial summation gives the second. The same binomial estimate gives $O(t/\log t)$ primes in a dyadic interval at scale $t$. Decomposing $[a,b]$ into multiplicative intervals and summing their reciprocal costs gives the first bound in <a href="#eq:prime-reciprocals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-reciprocals">[eq:prime-reciprocals]</a>, including the possible endpoint interval. The Euler product on its right expands into reciprocals of all positive integers with prime factors at most $z$, including every integer at most $z$.

For <a href="#eq:divisor-small-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:divisor-small-power">[eq:divisor-small-power]</a>, at all sufficiently large primes the factor $(j+1)^C$ is bounded by $p^{\epsilon j/2}$ for every $j\ge1$, and $R_p^{-C}\le p^{\epsilon/2}$. Each of the finitely many remaining primes has a bounded supremum after division by its positive exponential factor $p^{\epsilon j}$, or contributes a fixed constant in the $R(n)$ bound. Multiplication proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

We will use Cauchy–Schwarz, Hölder, the Chinese remainder theorem, Markov’s inequality, and finite orbit averaging. Size parameters and vertex totals in independent arithmetic estimates are local to those estimates; the centre and table notation is introduced only when needed.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### A tensor kernel for integer families

The centre construction will use a kernel bound for pairs of integer families. We derive it from a concentration observation on a single prime coordinate, retaining the concentration conclusions for the weighted lcm estimate in Section <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">6</a>. The diagonal-concentration argument is an adaptation of Green and Walker (Green and Walker 2021, Lemma 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[GW2021](#ref-GW2021)] and Hauke-Treuer, Vazquez and Walker (Hauke-Treuer, Vazquez, and Walker 2026, Lemma 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[HVW2026](#ref-HVW2026)]. We include the proof, together with the kernel-total and off-pivot estimates needed below. Exponent indices in this subsection belong to $\mathbb Z_{\ge0}$.

<a id="stmt-ar-pivot"></a>
<div id="ar:pivot" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (One-coordinate concentration). *Fix $1/2<u<1$ and $A\ge1$. For probability vectors $\alpha,\beta$ and $0<q<1/2$, put $$T_q(\alpha,\beta)=\sum_{i,j}
 q^{|i-j|}A^{\mathbf1_{i\ne j}}(\alpha_i\beta_j)^u.$$ Then $$T_q(\alpha,\beta)\le 1+O_{u,A}(q^{1/(1-u)}).$$ Moreover, if a probability array $(m_{ij})$ satisfies $$m_{ij}\le q^{|i-j|}A^{\mathbf1_{i\ne j}}
 (\alpha_i\beta_j)^u,$$ there is, for sufficiently small $q$, an index $k$ such that $$d:=(1-\alpha_k)+(1-\beta_k)\ll_{u,A}q^{1/(1-u)}.$$ For this index the mass on $i,j\ne k$ is $O_{u,A}(d^{2u})$, and the mass on pairs with exactly one index equal to $k$ and the other at distance at least $a\ge1$ is $O_{u,A}(q^a d^u)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ar-pivot"><summary>Proof of stmt-ar-pivot</summary>

*Proof.* The off-diagonal convolution kernel has $\ell^1$ norm $O_A(q)$, whereas $\|\alpha^u\|_2,\|\beta^u\|_2\le1$. Hence its contribution is $O_A(q)$. The diagonal contribution is at most $$\left(\sum_i\alpha_i^{2u}\sum_j\beta_j^{2u}\right)^{1/2}.$$ If the total is at least one, the latter expression is $1-O_A(q)$. Since $$\sum_i\alpha_i^{2u}\le(\max_i\alpha_i)^{2u-1},$$ both vectors have atoms of mass $1-o(1)$. These atoms have the same index: the actual diagonal sum is at least $1-O_A(q)$, whereas different dominant indices would bound it by $O(d_\alpha^u+d_\beta^u+(d_\alpha d_\beta)^u)=o(1)$, with $d_\alpha,d_\beta$ their off-atom masses. Call the common index $k$ and define $d$ as in the statement.

The central term is at most $1-c_ud$ when $d$ is small. The single-off terms are $O_{u,A}(qd^u)$ by geometric summation and Holder’s inequality. More generally, without any support-size assumption, $$\sum_{|j-k|\ge a}q^{|j-k|}\beta_j^u
 \le\left(\sum_{j\ne k}\beta_j\right)^u
 \left(\sum_{|j-k|\ge a}q^{|j-k|/(1-u)}\right)^{1-u}
 \ll_u q^a d^u.$$ The double-off terms are $O_{u,A}(d^{2u})$ by the convolution bound applied to the two off-parts. Consequently $$T_q(\alpha,\beta)
 \le 1-c_ud+O_{u,A}(d^{2u}+qd^u).$$ Absorb $d^{2u}$ using $2u>1$. Maximizing the remaining expression in $d$ proves the asserted excess bound. If a probability array is dominated by the kernel, its total is one, so the same inequality forces $d\ll q^{1/(1-u)}$. Restricting the single-off geometric sum to distances at least $a$ gives its last asserted bound. When the kernel total is less than one, the first conclusion is immediate. For $q$ in a compact subinterval $[q_0,1/2)$, the convolution norm is bounded by $1+2Aq/(1-q)\le1+2A$. Increasing the constant therefore extends the first bound to all $0<q<1/2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ar-tensor"></a>
<div id="ar:tensor" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Tensor kernel). *For $1/2<u<1$, $s>1-u$, and nonnegative sequences $(x_L),(y_M)$ on the positive integers, $$\sum_{L,M\ge1}
 \frac{(x_Ly_M)^u}{\bigl(L/(L,M)\cdot M/(L,M)\bigr)^s}
 \ll_{u,s}\left(\sum_Lx_L\sum_My_M\right)^u.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ar-tensor"><summary>Proof of stmt-ar-tensor</summary>

*Proof.* It suffices first to consider finite supports and positive totals, and normalize both totals to one. At each sufficiently large prime $p$, Lemma <a href="#ar:pivot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ar:pivot">7</a> with $q=p^{-s}$ bounds the optimal constant in the corresponding one-coordinate estimate by $1+O_{u,s}(p^{-s/(1-u)})$. Each of the finitely many small primes also has a bounded optimal constant, directly from the $\ell^1$ convolution bound.

To tensorize, fix the two exponents at the first prime and apply the remaining-coordinate inequality to the corresponding slices. Its right side is the product of their slice totals to the power $u$. The one-coordinate bound then sums those totals. Repeating this argument gives the product of these one-prime constants. This product converges because $s/(1-u)>1$. Zero totals are trivial, and monotone convergence removes the finite-support restriction. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Residue averages for rational grids

For positive integers $v,w$, consider the two lifted grids $(z+\xi)/v$ and $(z'+\xi')/w$, with fixed real phases and positive half-widths $r_1,r_2$. Put $$g=(v,w),\qquad s=w/g,\qquad t=v/g.$$ The integer determinant $n=sz-tz'$ records their relative positions. In particular, distance at most $C\max(r_1,r_2)$ restricts $n$ to an interval of length $O_C(\eta)$, where $\eta=[v,w]\max(r_1,r_2)$.

<a id="stmt-ar-cycle"></a>
<div id="ar:cycle" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Residue averages on determinant cycles). *Fix divisors $D_1\mid v$, $D_2\mid w$ and a nonnegative function of $z\bmod D_1,z'\bmod D_2$. At every integer determinant there are $g$ solutions per unit first-point translate; let $A(n)$ be their average value. Then $A$ has a period dividing $D_1D_2$, and its mean $\overline A$ is the independent uniform residue mean of the function. For any real interval $J$, $$\sum_{n\in J\cap\mathbb Z}A(n)
 =|J|\overline A+O(D_1D_2\overline A).$$ If one also requires $n\equiv n_0\pmod Q$ and $(Q,D_1D_2)=1$, the main term is $|J|\overline A/Q$ with the same error bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ar-cycle"><summary>Proof of stmt-ar-cycle</summary>

*Proof.* Since $(s,t)=1$, all solutions at a fixed determinant differ by $(t,s)$. Taking the first numerator over one period of length $v$ gives a cycle of length $g$. A Bezout solution for an increment of $D_1D_2$ in the determinant preserves both tested residues, proving periodicity. Averaging also over $n\bmod[v,w]$ enumerates all $vw$ pairs of numerator residues modulo $v,w$, proving the mean assertion.

A nonnegative periodic sequence of period $P$ and mean $\overline A$ has total mass $P\overline A$ in one period. Splitting an interval into whole periods and two end pieces therefore gives an error $O(P\overline A)$. Take $P=D_1D_2$. Finally, stepping by a number coprime to $P$ permutes its residues, so the same proof applies on the stated progression. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:centres"></a>
### Centres, density, and prime heights

Fix one of the finite blocks from Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>, of mass $w\le W\le 2w\le1$. All rows in this section belong to that block, and their positive spatial widths are exact dyadic numbers. We assign rows of one width to integer multiples of their denominators. The assignment will have two complementary properties: an assigned-row density bound at small spatial scale, and a quantitative separation between families assigned at widely different scales. These properties supply the summable centre kernels used in the later pair counts.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Popular multiples and the density bound

Fix $H_0=.001$. A small constant $k_0>0$ will be chosen after the other fixed parameters. Write $$W_r=\sum_{\substack{v\text{ in the block}\\r(v)=r}}\phi(v)r.$$

<a id="stmt-cen-popular"></a>
<div id="cen:popular" class="definition">

**Definition 10** (Popular multiples and original hosts). An integer $D\ge1$ is *popular at width $r$* if $Dr\le k_0$ and $$\sum_{\substack{v\mid D\\r(v)=r}}\frac{\phi(v)}D
 \ge (Dr)^{H_0}.$$ A row $v$ is *raw* if it divides no popular multiple at its width. Otherwise its *original host* is the largest popular multiple at that width divisible by $v$.

</div>

The largest host exists: at a fixed positive width, all popular multiples are at most $k_0/r$.

<a id="stmt-cen-density"></a>
<div id="cen:density" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:centre-density"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Density above original hosts). *Let $\mathcal V$ be a subfamily of rows of a common width $r$, all dividing an integer $L$. Suppose that $L$ is at least the original host of every nonraw row in $\mathcal V$; raw rows are allowed as well. Define $$k_L=Lr,\qquad
 \delta_L=\sum_{v\in\mathcal V}\frac{\phi(v)}L,\qquad
 m_L=k_L\delta_L.$$ Then $$\delta_L\ll_{k_0} k_L^{H_0}.
 \label{eq:centre-density}$$ Moreover $\delta_L\le1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-density</summary>

*Proof.* The last assertion follows from $\sum_{v\mid L}\phi(v)=L$. If the family is nonempty, $2L$ cannot be popular at width $r$: any assigned nonraw row would then have an original host at least $2L$, whereas any assigned raw row would no longer be raw. If $2Lr\le k_0$, failure of popularity gives $$\frac12\delta_L
 \le\sum_{\substack{v\mid2L\\r(v)=r}}\frac{\phi(v)}{2L}
 <(2Lr)^{H_0}.$$ If $2Lr>k_0$, use $\delta_L\le1$. The empty family is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:centre-kernel"></a>
A *centre family* consists of an integer $L$, a common width $r$, and an assigned subfamily satisfying the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#cen:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:density">11</a>. For two such families, indexed by $i,j$, use the notation $$L=L_i,\quad M=L_j,\quad G=(L,M),\quad U=L/G,\quad T=M/G,$$ $$\begin{gathered}
 r_{\min}=\min(r_i,r_j),\qquad r_{\max}=\max(r_i,r_j),\\
 \Theta_{ij}=G r_{\min},\qquad K=[L,M]r_{\max}.
 \end{gathered}$$ In particular $(U,T)=1$. The identity $$\Theta_{ij}
 =(m_Lm_M)^{1/2}(UT)^{-1/2}
 \left(\frac{r_{\min}}{r_{\max}}\right)^{1/2}
 (\delta_L\delta_M)^{-1/2}
 \label{eq:centre-kernel}$$ will convert arithmetic separation into a summable mass kernel. We apply it only to nonempty families, so its inverse densities are defined.

<a id="stmt-cen-kernel"></a>
<div id="cen:kernel" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Centre-kernel summation). *Consider centre families satisfying Lemma <a href="#cen:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:density">11</a>, with at most one family for each pair $(L,r)$ and total mass at most $W\le1$. For every fixed $\alpha>1/2$ and sufficiently small fixed $\epsilon>0$, there is $c>0$ such that $$\sum_{i,j}\Theta_{ij}(\delta_{L_i}\delta_{L_j})^\alpha
 (U_{ij}T_{ij})^\epsilon
 \ll_{k_0,\alpha,\epsilon} W^{1+c}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $u>1/2$ sufficiently close to $1/2$ that $H_0(\alpha-u)>u-1/2$, and then $\epsilon<u-1/2$. For a single centre, $$m_L^{1/2-u}\delta_L^{\alpha-1/2}
 =k_L^{1/2-u}\delta_L^{\alpha-u}\ll_{k_0}1.$$ Indeed use <a href="#eq:centre-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centre-density">[eq:centre-density]</a> when $k_L\le1$, and use $\delta_L\le1$ when $k_L>1$. Hence the summand is at most $$C_{k_0}(m_Lm_M)^u(UT)^{-1/2+\epsilon}
 \left(\frac{r_{\min}}{r_{\max}}\right)^{1/2}.$$ At each pair of exact widths Lemma <a href="#ar:tensor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ar:tensor">8</a> applies, since $1/2-\epsilon>1-u$. If $M_r$ is the total mass at width $r$, the remaining sum is bounded by $$C\sum_{r,s}(M_rM_s)^u
 \left(\frac{\min(r,s)}{\max(r,s)}\right)^{1/2}
 \ll\sum_r M_r^{2u}\le W^{2u}.$$ The middle inequality is convolution with a summable geometric kernel on dyadic width indices. Take $c=2u-1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cen-raw"></a>
<div id="cen:raw" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Raw rows suffice when they carry mass). *If raw rows have mass at least $w/4$ on arbitrarily late blocks, the positive-measure avoided set fixed in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> cannot exist.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-raw"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-raw</summary>

*Proof.* For a raw row use centre $L=v$ and give every point of its grid weight $R(v)=\phi(v)/v$. Then $\delta_L=R(v)$ and $m_L=\phi(v)r(v)$. The determinant-cycle count of Lemma <a href="#ar:cycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ar:cycle">9</a> at distance $O(r_{\max})$, multiplied by $r_{\min}$ and the two row weights, is $$O\bigl(m_Lm_M+\Theta_{ij}\delta_L\delta_M\bigr).$$ Lemma <a href="#cen:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:density">11</a> applies to these raw singleton families. Lemma <a href="#cen:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:kernel">12</a> with $\alpha=1$ therefore gives a uniform second-moment bound for their weighted interval trains. Their means are bounded below by a constant times $w$, and their integrals on each fixed interval approach that interval’s length times the mean: these are uniformly weighted grids with $v\to\infty$ down the block tail. Approximation of the avoided set by a finite union of intervals, using the second-moment bound for the approximation error, gives a positive integral on that set, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

We may consequently pass to arbitrarily late blocks on which the nonraw rows carry a fixed positive fraction of the block mass. The following adjustment of their hosts is made before any numerator weights are imposed.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Adjusted hosts and the density purge

The original hosts give the density bound, but they do not yet separate families assigned at different scales. We therefore allow a row to use a popular centre from another width, charging for the required arithmetic enlargement and width change. Minimizing this cost will ensure that a centre at a different scale cannot offer a substantially cheaper fit; Lemma <a href="#cen:exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:exterior">15</a> makes this separation precise.

<a id="cen:assignment-cost"></a>
For a nonraw row $v$ of width $r$, minimize over all original popular centres $(D,r_0)$ in the block the cost $$\begin{gathered}
 b+10a+10c_++(-c)_+,\\
 b=-\log(Dr_0),\quad
 a=\log\frac{v}{(v,D)},\quad c=\log\frac r{r_0},
 \end{gathered}
 \label{cen:assignment-cost}$$ where $t_+=\max(t,0)$. Choose a minimizer, resolving ties arbitrarily, and put $L=[D,v]=De^a$. Consolidate assignments with the same triple $$(L,r,B^*),\qquad B^*=\lfloor\text{minimum cost}\rfloor,$$ into one *centre object*; call the integer $B^*$ its *shell*. Write $i$ for such an object, and let $B_i$ be the dyadic power of two with $B_i\le B_i^*<2B_i$. We call $B_i$ its band. All costs, and hence all these bands, are large when $k_0$ is small.

For each object define $k_{L_i},\delta_{L_i},m_{L_i}$ as in Lemma <a href="#cen:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:density">11</a>, using precisely its assigned rows, and put $$\mathcal F_i=\{L_i/v:v\text{ is assigned to }i\}.$$ Thus $\pi_{L_i}(\mathcal F_i)=\delta_{L_i}$. Different objects partition the assigned rows, including their masses. These symbols will always refer to the original assignments, even when some masks or numerator points are subsequently removed.

<a id="stmt-cen-adjusted"></a>
<div id="cen:adjusted" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Scale and number of adjusted centres). *Every adjusted centre satisfies Lemma <a href="#cen:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:density">11</a> and $$|\log k_{L_i}|\le B_i^*+1,\qquad
 \log k_{L_i}\le .1(B_i^*+1).$$ The number of original popular centres at a fixed width $r$ and with $-\log(Dr)\in[b,b+1)$ is $$\ll W_r e^{(1+3H_0)b}.$$ The total number of adjusted centre objects with $B_i^*\le t$, including all widths, is $\ll e^{2t}$. Deleting objects with $\delta_{L_i}<e^{-.32B_i^*}$ loses $o(W)$ as $k_0\to0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-adjusted"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-adjusted</summary>

*Proof.* For the chosen candidate in <a href="#cen:assignment-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:assignment-cost">[cen:assignment-cost]</a>, $\log k_L=-b+a+c$. Since $b>0$ and $a\ge0$, the cost bounds $|\log k_L|$, and it is at least $10\log k_L$ when the latter is positive. The cost is no larger than the cost obtained by using the row’s original host at its own width, namely minus the log of that host’s spatial scale. Since the cost also bounds $-\log k_L$, the adjusted $L$ is at least that original host. This proves the first assertions.

Let $n$ be the number of original popular centres in the indicated unit $b$-range. Sum the popularity inequality after multiplying by $Dr$, and apply Cauchy–Schwarz to row incidences. Using $\sum_{v\mid g}\phi(v)=g$ gives $$\bigl(n e^{-(1+H_0)b}\bigr)^2
 \ll W_r\sum_{D,D'}(D,D')r.$$ Here $D,D'\asymp e^{-b}/r$, so Lemma <a href="#ar:tensor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ar:tensor">8</a> gives, for $u>1/2$ arbitrarily close to $1/2$, $$\sum_{D,D'}(D,D')r\ll e^{-b}n^{2u}.$$ Consequently $$n\ll W_r^{1/(2-2u)}
 \exp\left(\frac{1+2H_0}{2-2u}b\right)
 \ll W_r e^{(1+3H_0)b},$$ after choosing $u$ sufficiently close to $1/2$ and using $W_r\le1$.

At a fixed source width $r_0$, sort the assignments into unit boxes of $(b,a,c)$. For each source $D$, there are $O(e^a)$ possibilities for the integer $v/(v,D)$ and hence for $L$. A unit $c$-range contains only boundedly many dyadic widths, and a unit $(b,a,c)$-box gives boundedly many integer shells. Thus the number of resulting objects is $$\ll W_{r_0}\exp((1+3H_0)b+a).$$ Multiplication by $k_L=e^{-b+a+c}$ leaves the exponent $$3H_0b+2a+c\le .21B_i^*+O(1).$$ An object in the stated deletion contributes an additional factor $e^{-.32B_i^*}$. This bounds its entire mass, not just the mass of rows using the particular source witness. If an object has several source witnesses, cover it by all their boxes, or choose any one: the upper count can overcount objects but cannot omit one. The coefficient inequality above holds also for $c<0$, since then $c\le .21(-c)_+$. At shell $t$, the nonnegative cost bounds $b,a,|c|$ by $O(t+1)$, so there are $O((t+1)^3)$ unit boxes. All costs are at least $-\log k_0$. The deleted mass is therefore at most $$C\sum_{r_0}W_{r_0}
 \sum_{t\ge-\log k_0-O(1)}(t+1)^3e^{-.11t}=o(W).$$ This also shows uniformity when a source-width mass is arbitrarily small. Similarly, summing the object-count bound over cost at most $t$, and using $\sum_{r_0}W_{r_0}\le1$, gives $O(e^{2t})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:centre-lower"></a>
Retain the remaining objects. In addition to their upper density bound, their original assigned families satisfy the following lower bounds, before any subsequent mask or numerator removals: $$\delta_L\ge e^{-.32B_i^*},\qquad
 m_L\gg e^{-1.32B_i^*}.
 \label{eq:centre-lower}$$

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Exterior separation and summation with height decay

<a id="stmt-cen-exterior"></a>
<div id="cen:exterior" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:exterior-gain"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Separation of different bands). *If $B_i/B_j$ is sufficiently small, then every row $v$ assigned to $j$ satisfies at least one of $$\frac{v}{(v,L_i)}\frac{r_j}{r_{\min}}>e^{.015B_j^*},
 \qquad
 \log(r_i/r_j)>.7B_j^*.
 \label{eq:exterior-gain}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-exterior"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-exterior</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a source $(D_i,r_{0i})$ that produced an assignment in object $i$. Then $D_i\mid L_i$, and its source parameters in <a href="#cen:assignment-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:assignment-cost">[cen:assignment-cost]</a> are $O(B_i)$. In particular $$\log\frac{v}{(v,D_i)}
 \le \log\frac{v}{(v,L_i)}+\log\frac{L_i}{D_i}.$$ Using $(D_i,r_{0i})$ as a candidate for $v$, and comparing the two widths through $r_i$, bounds its minimum cost by $$O(B_i)+10\log\frac{v}{(v,L_i)}
 +10\log\frac{r_j}{r_{\min}}+\log^+\frac{r_i}{r_j}.$$ If both conclusions failed, this would be at most $O(B_i)+.85B_j^*<B_j^*$, contrary to the definition of the shell. ◻

</details>

We record two ways in which <a href="#eq:centre-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centre-kernel">[eq:centre-kernel]</a> will absorb errors. This also explains why the shell label in an adjusted centre does not invalidate the earlier tensor summations.

<a id="stmt-cen-decay-kernels"></a>
<div id="cen:decay-kernels" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Width and height gains). *Under <a href="#eq:centre-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centre-lower">[eq:centre-lower]</a>, the following kernels are summable with a positive power of total mass beyond linear. First, restrict to pairs with sufficiently small $B_i/B_j$ and $\log(r_i/r_j)>.65B_j^*$. For sufficiently small absolute $c_s,\epsilon>0$, the kernel is $$\Theta_{ij}e^{c_sB_j}(UT)^\epsilon.$$ Second, fix $c>0$ and $C>0$. Sum over dyadic $Y$ and pairs with $B_i,B_j\le Y/A_0$. For $A_0$ sufficiently large the kernel is $$\Theta_{ij}e^{-cY}\tau(UT)^C.$$ The bounds permit the shell multiplicities of the adjusted objects and the displayed height summation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-decay-kernels"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-decay-kernels</summary>

*Proof.* In <a href="#eq:centre-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centre-kernel">[eq:centre-kernel]</a>, changing the mass exponent from $1/2$ to $u>1/2$, including the inverse densities there, costs at most $$C\exp\left(
  \bigl(.16+1.32(u-1/2)\bigr)(B_i^*+B_j^*)\right).$$ For the first kernel, take $0<u-1/2\le .005$ and use band separation so that $B_i^*/B_j^*\le .01$. The displayed conversion cost is then at most $Ce^{.18B_j^*}$. Reserve the width power $1/8$ for summation. Its remaining power $3/8$, together with $\log(r_i/r_j)>.65B_j^*$, supplies $e^{-.24375B_j^*}$. For $c_s\le .01$ this leaves at least $e^{-.05B_j^*}$. Choose $\epsilon<u-1/2$, so the remaining $UT$-price still meets the tensor inequality.

At a fixed pair of integer shells $s,t$, there is at most one object at a given $(L,r)$. Tensor summation followed by convolution in the widths, now with their power $1/8$, bounds this pair of shells by $CW_s^uW_t^u\le CW^{2u}$, where $W_s,W_t$ are their total masses. Thus the first kernel is bounded by $$CW^{2u}\sum_{t\ge1}(t+1)e^{-.05t}\ll W^{2u}.$$ There is no summation of an undamped uniform shell bound.

For the second kernel take $u=.75$, and write $C_u=.16+1.32(u-1/2)$. Its shell-conversion cost is at most $C\exp(4C_uY/A_0)$, since $B_i^*,B_j^*<2Y/A_0$. Enlarge $A_0$ so that $4C_u/A_0<c/2$. The divisor bound $\tau(UT)^C\ll_\epsilon(UT)^\epsilon$ uses less than the available margin $u-1/2$ in the tensor inequality. At each pair of shells, tensor and width summation again cost at most $CW^{2u}$. There are $O((Y+1)^2)$ eligible shell pairs, so the total is at most $$CW^{2u}\sum_{Y\text{ dyadic}}(Y+1)^2e^{-cY/2}
 \ll W^{2u}.$$ Both exponents $2u$ are strictly greater than one. ◻

</details>

<a id="cen:height-starts"></a>
#### Prime heights and starting scales

Use dyadic heights $Y=1,2,4,\ldots$ for the prime bins $$\mathcal P(Y)=\{p:Y\le\log\log p<2Y\}.$$ Primes below these bins are always part of the low initial data. A bin is *dense on $L_i$* if $$\sum_{\substack{p\mid L_i\\p\in\mathcal P(Y)}}\frac1p
 \ge\kappa Y.$$ Let $Y_d(i)$ be the last dense height, or zero if there is none. The two types and their starts are defined by $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \text{type}&\text{condition}&Y_0(i)\\ \hline
 \text{high}&Y_d(i)>D_0B_i&2Y_d(i)\\
 \text{medium}&Y_d(i)\le D_0B_i&\max(A_0B_i,2Y_d(i)).
 \end{array}$$ All bins from the start onward are sparse. Colour the integers $\log_2B_i$ modulo a fixed large integer. Retain a single type and colour carrying the greatest mass. This keeps $\gg W$ mass, with a fixed implied constant, and makes distinct retained bands as widely separated as required below.

<a id="cen:hierarchy"></a>
#### Order of the fixed parameters

We give the order here because all subsequent selections must be uniform down the block tail. Some constants name thresholds that are used only later: their quantitative roles are specified below, and their constructions are given at the indicated sections. Every choice is fixed independently of the block. This is the dependency order of the constants, not the chronology of the adaptive construction; the list also serves as a reference for the later estimates.

1.  *Prime powers and geometry.* Choose first a sufficiently large absolute $C_{\rm mask}$, bounding the excess prime powers in a deficit mask. Choose a sufficiently large absolute $\beta$, which will join centres at height $Y$ when $\log UT\le\beta Y$. It must absorb the absolute prime-product costs $e^{CY}$ in the distant comparisons of Sections <a href="#sec:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:partitions">9</a> and <a href="#sec:joins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:joins">10</a>. Set $\sigma_g=.1$. Choose a small dyadic $\sigma>0$ so that absolute harmonic costs below height $\sigma Y$ are at most $e^{Y/100}$. Choose $$0<h_*\ll\sigma h_H,\qquad 0<h_H\ll\sigma,
     \qquad 3h_*<h_a\ll\sigma_g,$$ with arbitrarily large separations. Here $h_*$ controls the logarithmic sizes of comparison data within a component, $h_H$ controls the rational-model heights, and $h_a$ bounds the number of offset grids used to record very close pairs. Finally in this stage take the dyadic $A_0$ sufficiently large. In particular, path costs $e^{CY/A_0}$ must be at most $e^{h_*Y/10}$, and the height-decay applications of Lemma <a href="#cen:decay-kernels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:decay-kernels">16</a> must hold.

2.  *Occupancy and band separation.* Choose $s'>0$ sufficiently small absolutely for the later powers of divisor counts used with <a href="#eq:exterior-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exterior-gain">[eq:exterior-gain]</a>, then take $s_{\rm low}>0$ sufficiently smaller for the mask-moment deletion. Choose dyadic $\ell>0$ and $\kappa>0$ such that $\ell<\sigma$ and both $\ell$ and $\kappa A_0$ are arbitrarily small relative to $s_{\rm low}$. Next take dyadic $D_0$ large compared with $1/\kappa$ and $A_0$.
